People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

This indicates a mood or psychotic disorder with significant suicide risk. F32.3 and F33.3 (major depression with suicide risk) are primary considerations. F31.5-F31.6 (bipolar disorder with suicide risk) included given mood disturbance. F20.2 (schizophrenia with suicidal behavior) included as psychotic disorders can present with suicidality. Further assessment needed regarding mood symptoms, psychotic symptoms, substance use, and chronicity.",

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n2\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.3, F33.3, F31.5, F31.6, F20.2, F60.31\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well... I guess what finally scared me enough to make an appointment is that I just keep thinking it would be easier if I didn't wake up. It's just this heavy thought I can't shake. And... sometimes when I'm driving home from the clinic, I actually catch myself wondering what would happen if I just didn't turn the steering wheel. That really frightened me, so... that's why I'm here.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods lasting several days or longer where your mood was unusually high or expansive, where you felt like you could do anything, needed very little sleep, and your thoughts were racing?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Earlier this month, actually. I had a few days where I felt almost... electrically upbeat, I guess you could say. But it turned into me just snapping at everyone over really tiny things. Like, if a medication cup was misplaced at the clinic, or if there was a minor change in our morning schedule, I would just lose my temper. It really didn't feel like my usual self at all.\n\nDoctor: During that electrically upbeat period earlier this month, did you experience anything unusual like hearing voices, seeing things, or believing things that others didn't seem to believe—even if just for a moment?\n\nPatient: No, nothing like that. I didn't hear any voices or see anything that wasn't there. I didn't have any strange beliefs, either. I was just... really wired and angry, like I said. But my mind wasn't playing tricks on me or anything.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned feeling that heavy thought about not waking up and those intrusive thoughts while driving—are you still having those thoughts right now, or did they happen mainly during that 'electrically upbeat' period you described?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I'm having them right now. During that upbeat period earlier this month, I didn't feel this way at all—I was just really irritable and wired. The thoughts about not waking up, or the steering wheel... those are happening now. They've been pretty constant lately, which is why I'm sitting here today.\n\nDoctor: When did these thoughts about not waking up and the intrusive thoughts while driving first start—was that before the 'electrically upbeat' period earlier this month, or did they begin after that period ended?\n\nPatient: They started way before that upbeat period. Honestly, this heavy feeling... it's been hanging over me for about 11 months now. It's just that recently, after that wired phase ended, everything shifted and it's become completely unbearable. But yeah, the depression itself has been there for almost a year.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned the depression has been going on for about 11 months, and the wired, irritable period happened earlier this month. So just to be clear: was there a time when you felt normal or okay between when the depression started 11 months ago and when you had that wired period earlier this month, or has the heavy depressed feeling been pretty much continuous this whole time?\n\nPatient: No, there hasn't really been a break. It's just been one long stretch of feeling like this for the past 11 months. The only time it felt different was during those few days earlier this month when I got so wired and angry, but otherwise, yeah, it's just been a continuous heavy feeling the whole time.\n\nDoctor: During that wired and irritable period earlier this month, did you do things that were risky or that you wouldn't normally do—like spending a lot of money impulsively, taking on major projects, or engaging in other behaviors that had real consequences?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. I work as a vet tech, and during that wired stretch, I just... I got it in my head that I needed all this stuff for the clinic and for some rescues I foster. I bought all this monitoring equipment, um, ramps, specialized bedding, just... a bunch of supplies. It wasn't planned. It just felt urgently necessary right then, like I had to have it all immediately. I just put it all on my credit card. It was only after that phase ended that I looked at the statement and realized I'd racked up charges that my husband and I really can't cover.\n\nDoctor: During that wired period earlier this month when you were so irritable and energetic, did you feel like your thoughts were moving so fast that you couldn't keep up with them, or did you jump from topic to topic in a way that others had trouble following?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. My thoughts have been pretty grounded in reality, just... very negative.\n\nDoctor: During these 11 months of feeling heavy and depressed, have you had any beliefs about yourself—like thinking you're worthless, or that bad things are your fault, or that you deserve to suffer—that feel absolutely true to you, even though you know logically they might not be?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. Um... I just keep telling myself I'm a terrible person. We've had some disruption at home lately, and I just resent it so much, which makes me feel incredibly guilty. I keep replaying every impatient look I've given my husband, or any sharp comment I've made, and it just... it feels like proof that he and the rest of the family would be better off without me. I know it sounds harsh, but that's just how it feels.\n\nDoctor: During these 11 months when you've been feeling depressed and heavy, have there been any times when you felt like your thoughts or perceptions were distorted in some way—like feeling disconnected from your body, or experiencing things in a strange or unreal way, even if just briefly?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced anything like that. That really frightened me, so... that's why I'm here.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods lasting several days or longer where your mood was unusually high or expansive, where you felt like you could do anything, needed very little sleep, and your thoughts were racing?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Earlier this month, actually. I had a few days where I felt almost... electrically upbeat, I guess you could say. But it turned into me just snapping at everyone over really tiny things. Like, if a medication cup was misplaced at the clinic, or if there was a minor change in our morning schedule, I would just lose my temper. It really didn't feel like my usual self at all.\n\nDoctor: During that electrically upbeat period earlier this month, did you experience anything unusual like hearing voices, seeing things, or believing things that others didn't seem to believe—even if just for a moment?\n\nPatient: No, nothing like that. I didn't hear any voices or see anything that wasn't there. I didn't have any strange beliefs, either. I was just... really wired and angry, like I said. But my mind wasn't playing tricks on me or anything.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned feeling that heavy thought about not waking up and those intrusive thoughts while driving—are you still having those thoughts right now, or did they happen mainly during that 'electrically upbeat' period you described?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I'm having them right now. During that upbeat period earlier this month, I didn't feel this way at all—I was just really irritable and wired. The thoughts about not waking up, or the steering wheel... those are happening now. They've been pretty constant lately, which is why I'm sitting here today.\n\nDoctor: When did these thoughts about not waking up and the intrusive thoughts while driving first start—was that before the 'electrically upbeat' period earlier this month, or did they begin after that period ended?\n\nPatient: They started way before that upbeat period. Honestly, this heavy feeling... it's been hanging over me for about 11 months now. It's just that recently, after that wired phase ended, everything shifted and it's become completely unbearable. But yeah, the depression itself has been there for almost a year.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned the depression has been going on for about 11 months, and the wired, irritable period happened earlier this month. So just to be clear: was there a time when you felt normal or okay between when the depression started 11 months ago and when you had that wired period earlier this month, or has the heavy depressed feeling been pretty much continuous this whole time?\n\nPatient: No, there hasn't really been a break. It's just been one long stretch of feeling like this for the past 11 months. The only time it felt different was during those few days earlier this month when I got so wired and angry, but otherwise, yeah, it's just been a continuous heavy feeling the whole time.\n\nDoctor: During that wired and irritable period earlier this month, did you do things that were risky or that you wouldn't normally do—like spending a lot of money impulsively, taking on major projects, or engaging in other behaviors that had real consequences?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. I work as a vet tech, and during that wired stretch, I just... I got it in my head that I needed all this stuff for the clinic and for some rescues I foster. I bought all this monitoring equipment, um, ramps, specialized bedding, just... a bunch of supplies. It wasn't planned. It just felt urgently necessary right then, like I had to have it all immediately. I just put it all on my credit card. It was only after that phase ended that I looked at the statement and realized I'd racked up charges that my husband and I really can't cover.\n\nDoctor: During that wired period earlier this month when you were so irritable and energetic, did you feel like your thoughts were moving so fast that you couldn't keep up with them, or did you jump from topic to topic in a way that others had trouble following?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, my thoughts were definitely racing a bit, just because I was so agitated and focused on getting all those supplies, but I didn't feel like I couldn't keep up with them. And my husband didn't say anything about me not making sense or jumping around when I talked. I was just really intense and irritable about everything.\n\nDoctor: During these 11 months when you've been feeling that heavy depressed feeling, have you ever experienced any hallucinations, heard voices, or had beliefs that others didn't share—even if you knew they didn't make complete sense?\n\nPatient: No, nothing like that. Um... I just keep telling myself I'm a terrible person. We've had some disruption at home lately, and I just resent it so much, which makes me feel incredibly guilty. I know it sounds harsh, but that's just how it feels.\n\nDoctor: During these 11 months when you've been feeling depressed and heavy, have there been any times when you felt like your thoughts or perceptions were distorted in some way—like feeling disconnected from your body, or experiencing things in a strange or unreal way, even if just briefly?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced anything like that. Um... I definitely don't feel disconnected from my body. If anything, I just feel really weighed down by it. But no, I haven't had any moments where things seemed strange or unreal to me. Everything feels very real, just... really bleak.\n\nDoctor: During those 11 months when you've been feeling depressed and heavy, were there any times when you believed things about yourself or the world that felt absolutely real to you at the time, but that you now recognize didn't make sense—like feeling like you had special powers, or that people were against you, or that you had done something terribly wrong that you actually hadn't?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had anything like that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I haven't ever believed I was evil or committed an unforgivable sin or anything extreme like that. It's more just... this constant, heavy guilt. 

I keep telling myself I'm a terrible person because I resent how much disruption there is at home right now. I just sit there and replay every impatient look I've given, or every sharp comment I've made, and I use it as proof that my husband and my family would just be better off without me.
